But that incident was enough to make me think, “Something isn’t right with how I reacted.”\n\nDoctor: Since that day with the tablecloth incident, have you noticed any other changes in how you feel or act, especially things that feel out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not just that I snapped and then everything went back to normal. Since then, I haven’t really been keeping up with things the way I usually do.\n\nI help organize the Día de los Muertos event, and normally I’m very on top of that—lists, calls, making sure people know what they’re bringing, little details with the tables and decorations and all of that. I’ve done it enough times that people kind of expect me to be steady with it. But lately I just… I haven’t been able to keep up. I look at what needs doing and I don’t move through it the way I normally would. Things feel like they’re slipping, and that’s not like me.\n\nAnd I stopped going to card nights too. That’s something I usually enjoy, just sitting with people, talking, playing a few hands. But right now I don’t feel very steady around people. I don’t trust myself, I guess, after how I acted. I keep thinking, what if I get sharp with somebody again over nothing? So I’ve been staying away, which makes me feel a little ashamed, honestly, because those are my friends.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, don’t feel as rewarding or interesting to you lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it exactly that way. It’s hard to separate whether I’m not interested in them, or whether I’m just avoiding them because I don’t feel quite like myself.\n\nThe organizing—I still care about it. I know it matters, and I know people are counting on me. It’s not that I suddenly think, “Oh, who cares about the event?” I do care. But when I sit down to handle the details, it doesn’t feel satisfying the way it normally does. Usually I like getting things in order, crossing things off a list, making sure everything looks right. Lately it feels more like something I’m supposed to do, and I keep putting it off.\n\nAnd cards… I don’t know. I usually enjoy the company, the joking around, the routine of it. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped liking my friends or anything like that. It’s more that the thought of going doesn’t give me that little lift it usually does. It feels like effort. So yes, maybe they don’t feel as rewarding right now, but I’m not sure if that’s because I’ve lost interest or because I’m worried about how I’ll be around people.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the past two weeks when you didn’t take pleasure in things you usually enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards with your friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if it’s “not taking pleasure” exactly, or more that I’ve been hesitant to put myself in those situations.\n\nI mean, in the past couple of weeks, when I think about the event planning, I still know it matters to me. I still care about it being done well. But I can’t say it’s felt enjoyable in the usual way. Normally I get a kind of satisfaction from getting everything lined up and knowing people will have what they need. Lately, when I try to think about it, it just feels heavier, like something I should be doing.\n\nWith cards, I haven’t really felt that same pull to go, either. Usually I’d look forward to seeing everyone, hearing the same old jokes, that sort of thing. Recently, the thought of it hasn’t felt fun so much as… I don’t know, like effort. So yes, there have probably been times where I wasn’t taking the usual pleasure in those things, but I’m not sure whether that’s because I stopped enjoying them or because I’ve been holding back from them.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you found yourself not enjoying activities that you usually like, such as organizing events or playing cards with friends, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out that cleanly, doctor. That’s the trouble. When I think about cards, for example, almost right away the worry about being around people comes with it, so I don’t know what I’d feel if that wasn’t there.\n\nAnd with the event organizing, that’s not really about acting badly around others in the same way. I can be sitting by myself looking at a list, and it still doesn’t feel like it usually does. But I don’t know if I’d call that “not enjoying it,” exactly. It’s more like… the normal satisfaction is harder to get to. I still care about it, and I still want it done right. It just feels heavier than usual.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is, I haven’t really noticed a clear time where I thought, “I’m not enjoying this even though I’m not worried.” It all feels kind of mixed together right now.\n\nDoctor: When you’ve done the event planning or played cards in the past two weeks, have you noticed that you don’t get the same sense of enjoyment or pleasure from those activities as you used to, even when you’re not worried about how you might act around others?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that.\n\nWith the event planning, I’ve done little bits of it, but I can’t say I’ve really settled into it the way I normally would. Usually there’s a kind of good feeling in getting the details right, like, “All right, that’s handled.” Lately I don’t notice that feeling as much, but I also don’t know if that means I’m not enjoying it, or if I’m just distracted and not fully in it.\n\nAs for cards, I haven’t really had a moment where I thought, “I’m here, I’m relaxed, and I still don’t enjoy this.” If anything, I’m usually already thinking about whether I should go, how I’ll be, whether I’ll seem off. So it’s hard for me to separate the pleasure part from the worry part.\n\nSo I guess what I can say is: I haven’t noticed a clear, separate loss of enjoyment when the worry isn’t there. It’s more muddled than that for me right now.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say depressed most of the day, nearly every day. I’ve had low stretches, certainly, and some days where I feel more worn down or not quite like myself, but “down all day” sounds stronger than what I’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s more that my mood comes and goes. There are parts of the day where I’m quieter or I don’t feel as light as I usually might, but then something ordinary happens — a phone call, a task I need to do, even just getting through part of the day — and I’m not sitting there feeling depressed the whole time.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think it’s been most of the day nearly every day. At least, that’s not how I would describe it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past two weeks when you did something you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and you genuinely felt the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction you usually do?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and honestly… I can’t really put my finger on a clear example.\n\nThere have been small moments where I got something done with the event planning and thought, “Good, that’s taken care of,” but I don’t know if that was the same pleasure I usually get. It was more like relief that it wasn’t hanging over me. And with cards, I can’t say there’s been a time recently where I sat there and felt like my old self, just enjoying the game and the company without second-guessing it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to say no enjoyment at all, because that sounds too absolute, and I’m not sure that’s fair. But if you’re asking whether I can remember a definite moment in the last couple of weeks where I felt the same satisfaction or pleasure as before, no, I don’t think I can.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had any days where you genuinely didn’t feel like doing anything at all, even things you usually enjoy, not just because you were worried about how you might act, but because nothing seemed pleasurable or satisfying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve had a whole day like that, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I sit there and think, “I don’t really feel like doing much,” but when I try to look at why, it’s hard to say it’s because nothing would feel good. A lot of the time it’s more mixed up than that.\n\nThere have been mornings or parts of afternoons where I feel sort of flat or slow to get started, and I might put something off. But I can’t honestly say I had a day where I looked at everything — cards, planning, even ordinary things — and felt like none of it could be pleasurable or satisfying. It’s not that clear-cut for me.\n\nSo if you mean, “Did I have days where nothing at all seemed worth doing because there was no pleasure in anything?” I don’t think I’ve noticed that. At least not in a way I could confidently separate from just being unsettled or distracted.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you found yourself less interested in activities you usually enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that. I’ve been turning it over as you ask, because there are times when I don’t jump into things the way I normally might, but I can’t tell if that’s really less interest in the activity itself.\n\nWith the event organizing, for example, I still know it matters to me, and I still can get myself to think through what needs doing. I may not have that same easy enthusiasm right away, but I wouldn’t say I’m uninterested in it. And with cards, I don’t know… I haven’t noticed myself thinking, “I just don’t care about this anymore,” or anything like that.\n\nIf you mean when I’m completely setting aside any concern about being around people or how I’ll come across, then I honestly don’t know that I’ve identified a separate lack of interest. It feels muddled. I may hesitate or put something off, but I can’t confidently say it’s because the interest isn’t there. So I’d say I haven’t really noticed that clearly.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had times when activities you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, didn’t feel pleasurable or satisfying to you, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others or distracted by other thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m having trouble separating those things out, to be honest. When you say “even when you weren’t worried” and “not distracted by other thoughts,” that makes it harder for me to answer, because I don’t know that I’ve had many moments where everything else was completely out of the way and I could judge it cleanly.\n\nWith the event planning, there have been times where I did a task and it didn’t exactly feel satisfying in the old way, but I can’t say, “Oh, that was because the activity itself wasn’t pleasurable.” It might have just been that I was tired of thinking about it, or that I was doing it more out of habit. And with cards, I don’t think I’ve really tested it in a pure way, if that makes sense — like sitting down with no worries and no other thoughts and then noticing, “This isn’t fun.”\n\nSo I don’t think I can say yes in a definite way. I haven’t clearly noticed activities feeling flat or unsatisfying all by themselves. It’s more that I’m not sure what I’m feeling in the moment, and afterward I can’t always tell what part was enjoyment, what part was just getting through it, and what part was me being preoccupied.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I can’t think of a clear time like that, no. I’m trying to be careful, because I don’t want to just say no too quickly if there was some moment I’m forgetting. But when I look back over the last couple weeks, I don’t remember doing something I normally enjoy and thinking, “There’s absolutely nothing here for me, no pleasure, no satisfaction,” especially if you’re asking me to leave out being worried or distracted.

It was crooked, and I just blew up at him, much sharper than the situation called for.\n\nAnd the part that bothered me was how unlike me it felt. I don’t usually talk to people that way, especially not someone younger who was just trying to help. It wasn’t some big argument or anything important, which almost makes it worse in my mind. I keep thinking, “Why did I get so worked up over a tablecloth?” That’s what scared me a little, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself getting upset or reacting strongly to small things more often, even before the incident with the tablecloth?\n\nPatient: Before it? I… I can’t really say I had noticed a pattern, no. Not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think. What I keep coming back to is that one moment at the volunteer event, because it was so out of proportion.\n\nThe tablecloth was crooked—just crooked, not ruined, not causing any real problem—and one of the younger volunteers had put it down that way. And I just blew up at him. I spoke sharply, much more sharply than I should have. I don’t usually talk to people like that, especially not someone younger who’s there trying to help. That’s why it stuck with me so much. It felt like this flash of irritation came out before I had a chance to stop it, and then afterward I felt awful about it.\n\nSo, more often? I’m not sure I can honestly say that. But that incident was enough to make me think, “Something isn’t right with how I reacted.”\n\nDoctor: Since that day with the tablecloth incident, have you noticed any other changes in how you feel or act, especially things that feel out of character for you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. It’s not just that I snapped and then everything went back to normal. Since then, I haven’t really been keeping up with things the way I usually do.\n\nI help organize the Día de los Muertos event, and normally I’m very on top of that—lists, calls, making sure people know what they’re bringing, little details with the tables and decorations and all of that. I’ve done it enough times that people kind of expect me to be steady with it. But lately I just… I haven’t been able to keep up. I look at what needs doing and I don’t move through it the way I normally would. Things feel like they’re slipping, and that’s not like me.\n\nAnd I stopped going to card nights too. That’s something I usually enjoy, just sitting with people, talking, playing a few hands. But right now I don’t feel very steady around people. I don’t trust myself, I guess, after how I acted. I keep thinking, what if I get sharp with somebody again over nothing? So I’ve been staying away, which makes me feel a little ashamed, honestly, because those are my friends.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, don’t feel as rewarding or interesting to you lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it exactly that way. It’s hard to separate whether I’m not interested in them, or whether I’m just avoiding them because I don’t feel quite like myself.\n\nThe organizing—I still care about it. I know it matters, and I know people are counting on me. It’s not that I suddenly think, “Oh, who cares about the event?” I do care. But when I sit down to handle the details, it doesn’t feel satisfying the way it normally does. Usually I like getting things in order, crossing things off a list, making sure everything looks right. Lately it feels more like something I’m supposed to do, and I keep putting it off.\n\nAnd cards… I don’t know. I usually enjoy the company, the joking around, the routine of it. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped liking my friends or anything like that. It’s more that the thought of going doesn’t give me that little lift it usually does. It feels like effort. So yes, maybe they don’t feel as rewarding right now, but I’m not sure if that’s because I’ve lost interest or because I’m worried about how I’ll be around people.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the past two weeks when you didn’t take pleasure in things you usually enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards with your friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if it’s “not taking pleasure” exactly, or more that I’ve been hesitant to put myself in those situations.\n\nI mean, in the past couple of weeks, when I think about the event planning, I still know it matters to me. I still care about it being done well. But I can’t say it’s felt enjoyable in the usual way. Normally I get a kind of satisfaction from getting everything lined up and knowing people will have what they need. Lately, when I try to think about it, it just feels heavier, like something I should be doing.\n\nWith cards, I haven’t really felt that same pull to go, either. Usually I’d look forward to seeing everyone, hearing the same old jokes, that sort of thing. Recently, the thought of it hasn’t felt fun so much as… I don’t know, like effort. So yes, there have probably been times where I wasn’t taking the usual pleasure in those things, but I’m not sure whether that’s because I stopped enjoying them or because I’ve been holding back from them.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you found yourself not enjoying activities that you usually like, such as organizing events or playing cards with friends, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out that cleanly, doctor. That’s the trouble. When I think about cards, for example, almost right away the worry about being around people comes with it, so I don’t know what I’d feel if that wasn’t there.\n\nAnd with the event organizing, that’s not really about acting badly around others in the same way. I can be sitting by myself looking at a list, and it still doesn’t feel like it usually does. But I don’t know if I’d call that “not enjoying it,” exactly. It’s more like… the normal satisfaction is harder to get to. I still care about it, and I still want it done right. It just feels heavier than usual.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is, I haven’t really noticed a clear time where I thought, “I’m not enjoying this even though I’m not worried.” It all feels kind of mixed together right now.\n\nDoctor: When you’ve done the event planning or played cards in the past two weeks, have you noticed that you don’t get the same sense of enjoyment or pleasure from those activities as you used to, even when you’re not worried about how you might act around others?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that.\n\nWith the event planning, I’ve done little bits of it, but I can’t say I’ve really settled into it the way I normally would. Usually there’s a kind of good feeling in getting the details right, like, “All right, that’s handled.” Lately I don’t notice that feeling as much, but I also don’t know if that means I’m not enjoying it, or if I’m just distracted and not fully in it.\n\nAs for cards, I haven’t really had a moment where I thought, “I’m here, I’m relaxed, and I still don’t enjoy this.” If anything, I’m usually already thinking about whether I should go, how I’ll be, whether I’ll seem off. So it’s hard for me to separate the pleasure part from the worry part.\n\nSo I guess what I can say is: I haven’t noticed a clear, separate loss of enjoyment when the worry isn’t there. It’s more muddled than that for me right now.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say depressed most of the day, nearly every day. I’ve had low stretches, certainly, and some days where I feel more worn down or not quite like myself, but “down all day” sounds stronger than what I’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s more that my mood comes and goes. There are parts of the day where I’m quieter or I don’t feel as light as I usually might, but then something ordinary happens — a phone call, a task I need to do, even just getting through part of the day — and I’m not sitting there feeling depressed the whole time.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think it’s been most of the day nearly every day. At least, that’s not how I would describe it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past two weeks when you did something you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and you genuinely felt the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction you usually do?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and honestly… I can’t really put my finger on a clear example.\n\nThere have been small moments where I got something done with the event planning and thought, “Good, that’s taken care of,” but I don’t know if that was the same pleasure I usually get. It was more like relief that it wasn’t hanging over me. And with cards, I can’t say there’s been a time recently where I sat there and felt like my old self, just enjoying the game and the company without second-guessing it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to say no enjoyment at all, because that sounds too absolute, and I’m not sure that’s fair. But if you’re asking whether I can remember a definite moment in the last couple of weeks where I felt the same satisfaction or pleasure as before, no, I don’t think I can.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had any days where you genuinely didn’t feel like doing anything at all, even things you usually enjoy, not just because you were worried about how you might act, but because nothing seemed pleasurable or satisfying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve had a whole day like that, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I sit there and think, “I don’t really feel like doing much,” but when I try to look at why, it’s hard to say it’s because nothing would feel good. A lot of the time it’s more mixed up than that.\n\nThere have been mornings or parts of afternoons where I feel sort of flat or slow to get started, and I might put something off. But I can’t honestly say I had a day where I looked at everything — cards, planning, even ordinary things — and felt like none of it could be pleasurable or satisfying. It’s not that clear-cut for me.\n\nSo if you mean, “Did I have days where nothing at all seemed worth doing because there was no pleasure in anything?” I don’t think I’ve noticed that. At least not in a way I could confidently separate from just being unsettled or distracted.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you found yourself less interested in activities you usually enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that. I’ve been turning it over as you ask, because there are times when I don’t jump into things the way I normally might, but I can’t tell if that’s really less interest in the activity itself.\n\nWith the event organizing, for example, I still know it matters to me, and I still can get myself to think through what needs doing. I may not have that same easy enthusiasm right away, but I wouldn’t say I’m uninterested in it. And with cards, I don’t know… I haven’t noticed myself thinking, “I just don’t care about this anymore,” or anything like that.\n\nIf you mean when I’m completely setting aside any concern about being around people or how I’ll come across, then I honestly don’t know that I’ve identified a separate lack of interest. It feels muddled. I may hesitate or put something off, but I can’t confidently say it’s because the interest isn’t there. So I’d say I haven’t really noticed that clearly.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had times when activities you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, didn’t feel pleasurable or satisfying to you, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others or distracted by other thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m having trouble separating those things out, to be honest. When you say “even when you weren’t worried” and “not distracted by other thoughts,” that makes it harder for me to answer, because I don’t know that I’ve had many moments where everything else was completely out of the way and I could judge it cleanly.\n\nWith the event planning, there have been times where I did a task and it didn’t exactly feel satisfying in the old way, but I can’t say, “Oh, that was because the activity itself wasn’t pleasurable.” It might have just been that I was tired of thinking about it, or that I was doing it more out of habit. And with cards, I don’t think I’ve really tested it in a pure way, if that makes sense — like sitting down with no worries and no other thoughts and then noticing, “This isn’t fun.”\n\nSo I don’t think I can say yes in a definite way. I haven’t clearly noticed activities feeling flat or unsatisfying all by themselves. It’s more that I’m not sure what I’m feeling in the moment, and afterward I can’t always tell what part was enjoyment, what part was just getting through it, and what part was me being preoccupied.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time in the past two weeks when you did something you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and you didn’t feel any pleasure or satisfaction from it, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others or thinking about other things?\n\nPatient: I can’t think of a clear time like that, no. I’m trying to be careful, because I don’t want to just say no too quickly if there was some moment I’m forgetting. But when I look back over the last couple weeks, I don’t remember doing something I normally enjoy and thinking, “There’s absolutely nothing here for me, no pleasure, no satisfaction,” especially if you’re asking me to leave out being worried or distracted.\n\nThere have been moments where I was doing something and I wasn’t exactly lively about it, if that makes sense. Like I might go through the motions a bit, or not feel as eager as I used to right at the start. But even then, I can’t say there was no pleasure at all. Sometimes it’s more like the enjoyment is quieter, or I notice it only afterward, or I’m not sure what I felt because I wasn’t paying close attention to that part.\n\nSo, no, I don’t have a specific example where I did cards or planning or something like that and it gave me no satisfaction when nothing else was interfering. I just haven’t noticed it in that clean a way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think I’ve had that exact thought afterward. I mean, I’ve looked back on things and wondered whether I enjoyed them as much as I used to, but I haven’t had a clear moment where I said to myself, “That didn’t feel good at all.”

With organizing things, sometimes I’m more focused on just getting through the steps, making sure I haven’t forgotten anything, and then afterward I might feel a little worn out rather than pleased. But that’s not the same as thinking I got no enjoyment from it. And with cards, I can’t say I’ve finished and thought, “Well, that used to be fun and now it’s nothing.” It’s more vague than that.

So if you’re asking whether I’ve noticed a definite loss of pleasure in something I usually like, I’d say no, not really. I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say that.

When you say “even when you weren’t worried” and “not distracted by other thoughts,” that makes it harder for me to answer, because I don’t know that I’ve had many moments where everything else was completely out of the way and I could judge it cleanly.\n\nWith the event planning, there have been times where I did a task and it didn’t exactly feel satisfying in the old way, but I can’t say, “Oh, that was because the activity itself wasn’t pleasurable.” It might have just been that I was tired of thinking about it, or that I was doing it more out of habit. I haven’t clearly noticed activities feeling flat or unsatisfying all by themselves. It’s more that I’m not sure what I’m feeling in the moment, and afterward I can’t always tell what part was enjoyment, what part was just getting through it, and what part was me being preoccupied.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time in the past two weeks when you did something you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and you didn’t feel any pleasure or satisfaction from it, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others or thinking about other things?\n\nPatient: I can’t think of a clear time like that, no. I’m trying to be careful, because I don’t want to just say no too quickly if there was some moment I’m forgetting. Sometimes it’s more like the enjoyment is quieter, or I notice it only afterward, or I’m not sure what I felt because I wasn’t paying close attention to that part.\n\nSo, no, I don’t have a specific example where I did cards or planning or something like that and it gave me no satisfaction when nothing else was interfering. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way, no. I mean, “didn’t care about it as much” is maybe a little closer than “got no pleasure,” because there are times I’m not as eager or I don’t throw myself into things the way I might have before. But if you’re asking whether I did something I usually enjoy and, when I wasn’t worried or distracted, I could tell I just didn’t care about it anymore or cared a lot less… I can’t really point to that.

It’s more muddled than that for me. Sometimes I start something and I’m sort of slow to get into it, or I do it because it’s what I normally do, and then later I’m not sure how much I cared in the moment. But I haven’t had a definite thought like, “This doesn’t matter to me now,” or “I don’t care about this the way I used to.” I’d be guessing if I said yes to that.

So I guess my answer is no, not that I’ve clearly noticed. I can say I’ve been uncertain about how I’m feeling during things, but I haven’t identified a specific time where the interest itself was plainly gone.

I look at what needs doing and I don’t move through it the way I normally would. Things feel like they’re slipping, and that’s not like me.\n\nAnd I stopped going to card nights too. That’s something I usually enjoy, just sitting with people, talking, playing a few hands. But right now I don’t feel very steady around people. I don’t trust myself, I guess, after how I acted. I keep thinking, what if I get sharp with somebody again over nothing? So I’ve been staying away, which makes me feel a little ashamed, honestly, because those are my friends.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, don’t feel as rewarding or interesting to you lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it exactly that way. It’s hard to separate whether I’m not interested in them, or whether I’m just avoiding them because I don’t feel quite like myself.\n\nThe organizing—I still care about it. I know it matters, and I know people are counting on me. It’s not that I suddenly think, “Oh, who cares about the event?” I do care. But when I sit down to handle the details, it doesn’t feel satisfying the way it normally does. Usually I like getting things in order, crossing things off a list, making sure everything looks right. Lately it feels more like something I’m supposed to do, and I keep putting it off.\n\nAnd cards… I don’t know. I usually enjoy the company, the joking around, the routine of it. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped liking my friends or anything like that. It’s more that the thought of going doesn’t give me that little lift it usually does. It feels like effort. So yes, maybe they don’t feel as rewarding right now, but I’m not sure if that’s because I’ve lost interest or because I’m worried about how I’ll be around people.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the past two weeks when you didn’t take pleasure in things you usually enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards with your friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if it’s “not taking pleasure” exactly, or more that I’ve been hesitant to put myself in those situations.\n\nI mean, in the past couple of weeks, when I think about the event planning, I still know it matters to me. I still care about it being done well. But I can’t say it’s felt enjoyable in the usual way. Normally I get a kind of satisfaction from getting everything lined up and knowing people will have what they need. Lately, when I try to think about it, it just feels heavier, like something I should be doing.\n\nWith cards, I haven’t really felt that same pull to go, either. Usually I’d look forward to seeing everyone, hearing the same old jokes, that sort of thing. Recently, the thought of it hasn’t felt fun so much as… I don’t know, like effort. So yes, there have probably been times where I wasn’t taking the usual pleasure in those things, but I’m not sure whether that’s because I stopped enjoying them or because I’ve been holding back from them.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you found yourself not enjoying activities that you usually like, such as organizing events or playing cards with friends, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out that cleanly, doctor. That’s the trouble. When I think about cards, for example, almost right away the worry about being around people comes with it, so I don’t know what I’d feel if that wasn’t there.\n\nAnd with the event organizing, that’s not really about acting badly around others in the same way. I can be sitting by myself looking at a list, and it still doesn’t feel like it usually does. But I don’t know if I’d call that “not enjoying it,” exactly. It’s more like… the normal satisfaction is harder to get to. I still care about it, and I still want it done right. It just feels heavier than usual.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is, I haven’t really noticed a clear time where I thought, “I’m not enjoying this even though I’m not worried.” It all feels kind of mixed together right now.\n\nDoctor: When you’ve done the event planning or played cards in the past two weeks, have you noticed that you don’t get the same sense of enjoyment or pleasure from those activities as you used to, even when you’re not worried about how you might act around others?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that.\n\nWith the event planning, I’ve done little bits of it, but I can’t say I’ve really settled into it the way I normally would. Usually there’s a kind of good feeling in getting the details right, like, “All right, that’s handled.” Lately I don’t notice that feeling as much, but I also don’t know if that means I’m not enjoying it, or if I’m just distracted and not fully in it.\n\nAs for cards, I haven’t really had a moment where I thought, “I’m here, I’m relaxed, and I still don’t enjoy this.” If anything, I’m usually already thinking about whether I should go, how I’ll be, whether I’ll seem off. So it’s hard for me to separate the pleasure part from the worry part.\n\nSo I guess what I can say is: I haven’t noticed a clear, separate loss of enjoyment when the worry isn’t there. It’s more muddled than that for me right now.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say depressed most of the day, nearly every day. I’ve had low stretches, certainly, and some days where I feel more worn down or not quite like myself, but “down all day” sounds stronger than what I’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s more that my mood comes and goes. There are parts of the day where I’m quieter or I don’t feel as light as I usually might, but then something ordinary happens — a phone call, a task I need to do, even just getting through part of the day — and I’m not sitting there feeling depressed the whole time.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think it’s been most of the day nearly every day. At least, that’s not how I would describe it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past two weeks when you did something you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and you genuinely felt the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction you usually do?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and honestly… I can’t really put my finger on a clear example.\n\nThere have been small moments where I got something done with the event planning and thought, “Good, that’s taken care of,” but I don’t know if that was the same pleasure I usually get. It was more like relief that it wasn’t hanging over me. And with cards, I can’t say there’s been a time recently where I sat there and felt like my old self, just enjoying the game and the company without second-guessing it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to say no enjoyment at all, because that sounds too absolute, and I’m not sure that’s fair. But if you’re asking whether I can remember a definite moment in the last couple of weeks where I felt the same satisfaction or pleasure as before, no, I don’t think I can.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had any days where you genuinely didn’t feel like doing anything at all, even things you usually enjoy, not just because you were worried about how you might act, but because nothing seemed pleasurable or satisfying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve had a whole day like that, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I sit there and think, “I don’t really feel like doing much,” but when I try to look at why, it’s hard to say it’s because nothing would feel good. A lot of the time it’s more mixed up than that.\n\nThere have been mornings or parts of afternoons where I feel sort of flat or slow to get started, and I might put something off. But I can’t honestly say I had a day where I looked at everything — cards, planning, even ordinary things — and felt like none of it could be pleasurable or satisfying. It’s not that clear-cut for me.\n\nSo if you mean, “Did I have days where nothing at all seemed worth doing because there was no pleasure in anything?” I don’t think I’ve noticed that. At least not in a way I could confidently separate from just being unsettled or distracted.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you found yourself less interested in activities you usually enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that. I’ve been turning it over as you ask, because there are times when I don’t jump into things the way I normally might, but I can’t tell if that’s really less interest in the activity itself.\n\nWith the event organizing, for example, I still know it matters to me, and I still can get myself to think through what needs doing. I may not have that same easy enthusiasm right away, but I wouldn’t say I’m uninterested in it. And with cards, I don’t know… I haven’t noticed myself thinking, “I just don’t care about this anymore,” or anything like that.\n\nIf you mean when I’m completely setting aside any concern about being around people or how I’ll come across, then I honestly don’t know that I’ve identified a separate lack of interest. It feels muddled. I may hesitate or put something off, but I can’t confidently say it’s because the interest isn’t there. So I’d say I haven’t really noticed that clearly.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had times when activities you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, didn’t feel pleasurable or satisfying to you, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others or distracted by other thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m having trouble separating those things out, to be honest. When you say “even when you weren’t worried” and “not distracted by other thoughts,” that makes it harder for me to answer, because I don’t know that I’ve had many moments where everything else was completely out of the way and I could judge it cleanly.\n\nWith the event planning, there have been times where I did a task and it didn’t exactly feel satisfying in the old way, but I can’t say, “Oh, that was because the activity itself wasn’t pleasurable.” It might have just been that I was tired of thinking about it, or that I was doing it more out of habit. And with cards, I don’t think I’ve really tested it in a pure way, if that makes sense — like sitting down with no worries and no other thoughts and then noticing, “This isn’t fun.”\n\nSo I don’t think I can say yes in a definite way. I haven’t clearly noticed activities feeling flat or unsatisfying all by themselves. It’s more that I’m not sure what I’m feeling in the moment, and afterward I can’t always tell what part was enjoyment, what part was just getting through it, and what part was me being preoccupied.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time in the past two weeks when you did something you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and you didn’t feel any pleasure or satisfaction from it, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others or thinking about other things?\n\nPatient: I can’t think of a clear time like that, no. I’m trying to be careful, because I don’t want to just say no too quickly if there was some moment I’m forgetting. But when I look back over the last couple weeks, I don’t remember doing something I normally enjoy and thinking, “There’s absolutely nothing here for me, no pleasure, no satisfaction,” especially if you’re asking me to leave out being worried or distracted.\n\nThere have been moments where I was doing something and I wasn’t exactly lively about it, if that makes sense. Like I might go through the motions a bit, or not feel as eager as I used to right at the start. But even then, I can’t say there was no pleasure at all. Sometimes it’s more like the enjoyment is quieter, or I notice it only afterward, or I’m not sure what I felt because I wasn’t paying close attention to that part.\n\nSo, no, I don’t have a specific example where I did cards or planning or something like that and it gave me no satisfaction when nothing else was interfering. I just haven’t noticed it in that clean a way.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, has there been a time when you did something you usually enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and afterward you thought to yourself, 'That didn’t feel good at all, even though I used to really like doing it'?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that exact thought afterward. I mean, I’ve looked back on things and wondered whether I enjoyed them as much as I used to, but I haven’t had a clear moment where I said to myself, “That didn’t feel good at all.”\n\nWith organizing things, sometimes I’m more focused on just getting through the steps, making sure I haven’t forgotten anything, and then afterward I might feel a little worn out rather than pleased. But that’s not the same as thinking I got no enjoyment from it. And with cards, I can’t say I’ve finished and thought, “Well, that used to be fun and now it’s nothing.” It’s more vague than that.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether I’ve noticed a definite loss of pleasure in something I usually like, I’d say no, not really. I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you done something you used to really enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and noticed that you didn’t care about it as much as you used to, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others or thinking about something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way, no. I mean, “didn’t care about it as much” is maybe a little closer than “got no pleasure,” because there are times I’m not as eager or I don’t throw myself into things the way I might have before. But if you’re asking whether I did something I usually enjoy and, when I wasn’t worried or distracted, I could tell I just didn’t care about it anymore or cared a lot less… I can’t really point to that.\n\nIt’s more muddled than that for me. Sometimes I start something and I’m sort of slow to get into it, or I do it because it’s what I normally do, and then later I’m not sure how much I cared in the moment. But I haven’t had a definite thought like, “This doesn’t matter to me now,” or “I don’t care about this the way I used to.” I’d be guessing if I said yes to that.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not that I’ve clearly noticed.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’d put it that strongly, no. “Completely uninteresting or empty” doesn’t really fit what I’ve noticed. I mean, there are times lately where I’m not as sparked up about things as I might have been before, or I’m slower to settle into them, but I can’t honestly say I’ve sat there doing something I normally like and felt, “This is empty, there’s nothing in it for me.”

And I know you’re making the point about not counting times when I’m distracted or worrying, and that’s part of why I’m hesitant to say yes. If my mind is elsewhere, then of course things don’t feel the same. But when I try to separate that out, I haven’t clearly noticed that the activity itself has gone flat or meaningless.

So… no, I haven’t found that things I used to enjoy feel completely uninteresting or empty in that way. It’s more like I’m not always sure how much I’m enjoying something while it’s happening, but I wouldn’t call it gone.

There are parts of the day where I’m quieter or I don’t feel as light as I usually might, but then something ordinary happens — a phone call, a task I need to do, even just getting through part of the day — and I’m not sitting there feeling depressed the whole time.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think it’s been most of the day nearly every day. At least, that’s not how I would describe it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past two weeks when you did something you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and you genuinely felt the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction you usually do?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and honestly… I can’t really put my finger on a clear example.\n\nThere have been small moments where I got something done with the event planning and thought, “Good, that’s taken care of,” but I don’t know if that was the same pleasure I usually get. It was more like relief that it wasn’t hanging over me. And with cards, I can’t say there’s been a time recently where I sat there and felt like my old self, just enjoying the game and the company without second-guessing it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to say no enjoyment at all, because that sounds too absolute, and I’m not sure that’s fair. But if you’re asking whether I can remember a definite moment in the last couple of weeks where I felt the same satisfaction or pleasure as before, no, I don’t think I can.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had any days where you genuinely didn’t feel like doing anything at all, even things you usually enjoy, not just because you were worried about how you might act, but because nothing seemed pleasurable or satisfying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve had a whole day like that, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I sit there and think, “I don’t really feel like doing much,” but when I try to look at why, it’s hard to say it’s because nothing would feel good. A lot of the time it’s more mixed up than that.\n\nThere have been mornings or parts of afternoons where I feel sort of flat or slow to get started, and I might put something off. But I can’t honestly say I had a day where I looked at everything — cards, planning, even ordinary things — and felt like none of it could be pleasurable or satisfying. It’s not that clear-cut for me.\n\nSo if you mean, “Did I have days where nothing at all seemed worth doing because there was no pleasure in anything?” I don’t think I’ve noticed that. At least not in a way I could confidently separate from just being unsettled or distracted.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you found yourself less interested in activities you usually enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that. I’ve been turning it over as you ask, because there are times when I don’t jump into things the way I normally might, but I can’t tell if that’s really less interest in the activity itself.\n\nWith the event organizing, for example, I still know it matters to me, and I still can get myself to think through what needs doing. I may not have that same easy enthusiasm right away, but I wouldn’t say I’m uninterested in it. And with cards, I don’t know… I haven’t noticed myself thinking, “I just don’t care about this anymore,” or anything like that.\n\nIf you mean when I’m completely setting aside any concern about being around people or how I’ll come across, then I honestly don’t know that I’ve identified a separate lack of interest. It feels muddled. I may hesitate or put something off, but I can’t confidently say it’s because the interest isn’t there. So I’d say I haven’t really noticed that clearly.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had times when activities you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, didn’t feel pleasurable or satisfying to you, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others or distracted by other thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m having trouble separating those things out, to be honest. When you say “even when you weren’t worried” and “not distracted by other thoughts,” that makes it harder for me to answer, because I don’t know that I’ve had many moments where everything else was completely out of the way and I could judge it cleanly.\n\nWith the event planning, there have been times where I did a task and it didn’t exactly feel satisfying in the old way, but I can’t say, “Oh, that was because the activity itself wasn’t pleasurable.” It might have just been that I was tired of thinking about it, or that I was doing it more out of habit. And with cards, I don’t think I’ve really tested it in a pure way, if that makes sense — like sitting down with no worries and no other thoughts and then noticing, “This isn’t fun.”\n\nSo I don’t think I can say yes in a definite way. I haven’t clearly noticed activities feeling flat or unsatisfying all by themselves. It’s more that I’m not sure what I’m feeling in the moment, and afterward I can’t always tell what part was enjoyment, what part was just getting through it, and what part was me being preoccupied.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time in the past two weeks when you did something you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and you didn’t feel any pleasure or satisfaction from it, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others or thinking about other things?\n\nPatient: I can’t think of a clear time like that, no. I’m trying to be careful, because I don’t want to just say no too quickly if there was some moment I’m forgetting. But when I look back over the last couple weeks, I don’t remember doing something I normally enjoy and thinking, “There’s absolutely nothing here for me, no pleasure, no satisfaction,” especially if you’re asking me to leave out being worried or distracted.\n\nThere have been moments where I was doing something and I wasn’t exactly lively about it, if that makes sense. Like I might go through the motions a bit, or not feel as eager as I used to right at the start. But even then, I can’t say there was no pleasure at all. Sometimes it’s more like the enjoyment is quieter, or I notice it only afterward, or I’m not sure what I felt because I wasn’t paying close attention to that part.\n\nSo, no, I don’t have a specific example where I did cards or planning or something like that and it gave me no satisfaction when nothing else was interfering. I just haven’t noticed it in that clean a way.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, has there been a time when you did something you usually enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and afterward you thought to yourself, 'That didn’t feel good at all, even though I used to really like doing it'?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that exact thought afterward. I mean, I’ve looked back on things and wondered whether I enjoyed them as much as I used to, but I haven’t had a clear moment where I said to myself, “That didn’t feel good at all.”\n\nWith organizing things, sometimes I’m more focused on just getting through the steps, making sure I haven’t forgotten anything, and then afterward I might feel a little worn out rather than pleased. But that’s not the same as thinking I got no enjoyment from it. And with cards, I can’t say I’ve finished and thought, “Well, that used to be fun and now it’s nothing.” It’s more vague than that.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether I’ve noticed a definite loss of pleasure in something I usually like, I’d say no, not really. I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you done something you used to really enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and noticed that you didn’t care about it as much as you used to, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others or thinking about something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way, no. I mean, “didn’t care about it as much” is maybe a little closer than “got no pleasure,” because there are times I’m not as eager or I don’t throw myself into things the way I might have before. But if you’re asking whether I did something I usually enjoy and, when I wasn’t worried or distracted, I could tell I just didn’t care about it anymore or cared a lot less… I can’t really point to that.\n\nIt’s more muddled than that for me. Sometimes I start something and I’m sort of slow to get into it, or I do it because it’s what I normally do, and then later I’m not sure how much I cared in the moment. But I haven’t had a definite thought like, “This doesn’t matter to me now,” or “I don’t care about this the way I used to.” I’d be guessing if I said yes to that.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not that I’ve clearly noticed. No, I don’t think so—not in that definite way you’re describing. I know we’ve circled around this a bit, but “completely flat” or “no spark or joy at all” feels stronger than what I can honestly say.

There have been moments where I’m not as lively about things, maybe I go through the motions a little more, or I don’t notice enjoyment right away the way I might have before. But when I try to separate that from being preoccupied or just tired or having my mind elsewhere, I can’t point to something I usually enjoy and say, “There was nothing there at all.” It’s more uncertain than that.

So my answer would still be no, I haven’t clearly noticed that. I don’t want to overstate it just because I’m not feeling quite like myself during some things.

It’s not just that I snapped and then everything went back to normal. Since then, I haven’t really been keeping up with things the way I usually do.\n\nI help organize the Día de los Muertos event, and normally I’m very on top of that—lists, calls, making sure people know what they’re bringing, little details with the tables and decorations and all of that. I’ve done it enough times that people kind of expect me to be steady with it. But lately I just… I haven’t been able to keep up. I look at what needs doing and I don’t move through it the way I normally would. Things feel like they’re slipping, and that’s not like me.\n\nAnd I stopped going to card nights too. That’s something I usually enjoy, just sitting with people, talking, playing a few hands. But right now I don’t feel very steady around people. I don’t trust myself, I guess, after how I acted. I keep thinking, what if I get sharp with somebody again over nothing? So I’ve been staying away, which makes me feel a little ashamed, honestly, because those are my friends.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, don’t feel as rewarding or interesting to you lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it exactly that way. It’s hard to separate whether I’m not interested in them, or whether I’m just avoiding them because I don’t feel quite like myself.\n\nThe organizing—I still care about it. I know it matters, and I know people are counting on me. It’s not that I suddenly think, “Oh, who cares about the event?” I do care. But when I sit down to handle the details, it doesn’t feel satisfying the way it normally does. Usually I like getting things in order, crossing things off a list, making sure everything looks right. Lately it feels more like something I’m supposed to do, and I keep putting it off.\n\nAnd cards… I don’t know. I usually enjoy the company, the joking around, the routine of it. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped liking my friends or anything like that. It’s more that the thought of going doesn’t give me that little lift it usually does. It feels like effort. So yes, maybe they don’t feel as rewarding right now, but I’m not sure if that’s because I’ve lost interest or because I’m worried about how I’ll be around people.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time in the past two weeks when you didn’t take pleasure in things you usually enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards with your friends?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because I don’t know if it’s “not taking pleasure” exactly, or more that I’ve been hesitant to put myself in those situations.\n\nI mean, in the past couple of weeks, when I think about the event planning, I still know it matters to me. I still care about it being done well. But I can’t say it’s felt enjoyable in the usual way. Normally I get a kind of satisfaction from getting everything lined up and knowing people will have what they need. Lately, when I try to think about it, it just feels heavier, like something I should be doing.\n\nWith cards, I haven’t really felt that same pull to go, either. Usually I’d look forward to seeing everyone, hearing the same old jokes, that sort of thing. Recently, the thought of it hasn’t felt fun so much as… I don’t know, like effort. So yes, there have probably been times where I wasn’t taking the usual pleasure in those things, but I’m not sure whether that’s because I stopped enjoying them or because I’ve been holding back from them.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you found yourself not enjoying activities that you usually like, such as organizing events or playing cards with friends, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out that cleanly, doctor. That’s the trouble. When I think about cards, for example, almost right away the worry about being around people comes with it, so I don’t know what I’d feel if that wasn’t there.\n\nAnd with the event organizing, that’s not really about acting badly around others in the same way. I can be sitting by myself looking at a list, and it still doesn’t feel like it usually does. But I don’t know if I’d call that “not enjoying it,” exactly. It’s more like… the normal satisfaction is harder to get to. I still care about it, and I still want it done right. It just feels heavier than usual.\n\nSo I guess my honest answer is, I haven’t really noticed a clear time where I thought, “I’m not enjoying this even though I’m not worried.” It all feels kind of mixed together right now.\n\nDoctor: When you’ve done the event planning or played cards in the past two weeks, have you noticed that you don’t get the same sense of enjoyment or pleasure from those activities as you used to, even when you’re not worried about how you might act around others?\n\nPatient: I’m still not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that.\n\nWith the event planning, I’ve done little bits of it, but I can’t say I’ve really settled into it the way I normally would. Usually there’s a kind of good feeling in getting the details right, like, “All right, that’s handled.” Lately I don’t notice that feeling as much, but I also don’t know if that means I’m not enjoying it, or if I’m just distracted and not fully in it.\n\nAs for cards, I haven’t really had a moment where I thought, “I’m here, I’m relaxed, and I still don’t enjoy this.” If anything, I’m usually already thinking about whether I should go, how I’ll be, whether I’ll seem off. So it’s hard for me to separate the pleasure part from the worry part.\n\nSo I guess what I can say is: I haven’t noticed a clear, separate loss of enjoyment when the worry isn’t there. It’s more muddled than that for me right now.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say depressed most of the day, nearly every day. I’ve had low stretches, certainly, and some days where I feel more worn down or not quite like myself, but “down all day” sounds stronger than what I’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s more that my mood comes and goes. There are parts of the day where I’m quieter or I don’t feel as light as I usually might, but then something ordinary happens — a phone call, a task I need to do, even just getting through part of the day — and I’m not sitting there feeling depressed the whole time.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think it’s been most of the day nearly every day. At least, that’s not how I would describe it.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time in the past two weeks when you did something you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and you genuinely felt the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction you usually do?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think of one, and honestly… I can’t really put my finger on a clear example.\n\nThere have been small moments where I got something done with the event planning and thought, “Good, that’s taken care of,” but I don’t know if that was the same pleasure I usually get. It was more like relief that it wasn’t hanging over me. And with cards, I can’t say there’s been a time recently where I sat there and felt like my old self, just enjoying the game and the company without second-guessing it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to say no enjoyment at all, because that sounds too absolute, and I’m not sure that’s fair. But if you’re asking whether I can remember a definite moment in the last couple of weeks where I felt the same satisfaction or pleasure as before, no, I don’t think I can.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had any days where you genuinely didn’t feel like doing anything at all, even things you usually enjoy, not just because you were worried about how you might act, but because nothing seemed pleasurable or satisfying?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve had a whole day like that, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I sit there and think, “I don’t really feel like doing much,” but when I try to look at why, it’s hard to say it’s because nothing would feel good. A lot of the time it’s more mixed up than that.\n\nThere have been mornings or parts of afternoons where I feel sort of flat or slow to get started, and I might put something off. But I can’t honestly say I had a day where I looked at everything — cards, planning, even ordinary things — and felt like none of it could be pleasurable or satisfying. It’s not that clear-cut for me.\n\nSo if you mean, “Did I have days where nothing at all seemed worth doing because there was no pleasure in anything?” I don’t think I’ve noticed that. At least not in a way I could confidently separate from just being unsettled or distracted.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you found yourself less interested in activities you usually enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a clean yes or no on that. I’ve been turning it over as you ask, because there are times when I don’t jump into things the way I normally might, but I can’t tell if that’s really less interest in the activity itself.\n\nWith the event organizing, for example, I still know it matters to me, and I still can get myself to think through what needs doing. I may not have that same easy enthusiasm right away, but I wouldn’t say I’m uninterested in it. And with cards, I don’t know… I haven’t noticed myself thinking, “I just don’t care about this anymore,” or anything like that.\n\nIf you mean when I’m completely setting aside any concern about being around people or how I’ll come across, then I honestly don’t know that I’ve identified a separate lack of interest. It feels muddled. I may hesitate or put something off, but I can’t confidently say it’s because the interest isn’t there. So I’d say I haven’t really noticed that clearly.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had times when activities you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, didn’t feel pleasurable or satisfying to you, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others or distracted by other thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m having trouble separating those things out, to be honest. When you say “even when you weren’t worried” and “not distracted by other thoughts,” that makes it harder for me to answer, because I don’t know that I’ve had many moments where everything else was completely out of the way and I could judge it cleanly.\n\nWith the event planning, there have been times where I did a task and it didn’t exactly feel satisfying in the old way, but I can’t say, “Oh, that was because the activity itself wasn’t pleasurable.” It might have just been that I was tired of thinking about it, or that I was doing it more out of habit. And with cards, I don’t think I’ve really tested it in a pure way, if that makes sense — like sitting down with no worries and no other thoughts and then noticing, “This isn’t fun.”\n\nSo I don’t think I can say yes in a definite way. I haven’t clearly noticed activities feeling flat or unsatisfying all by themselves. It’s more that I’m not sure what I’m feeling in the moment, and afterward I can’t always tell what part was enjoyment, what part was just getting through it, and what part was me being preoccupied.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time in the past two weeks when you did something you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and you didn’t feel any pleasure or satisfaction from it, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others or thinking about other things?\n\nPatient: I can’t think of a clear time like that, no. But when I look back over the last couple weeks, I don’t remember doing something I normally enjoy and thinking, “There’s absolutely nothing here for me, no pleasure, no satisfaction,” especially if you’re asking me to leave out being worried or distracted.\n\nThere have been moments where I was doing something and I wasn’t exactly lively about it, if that makes sense. Like I might go through the motions a bit, or not feel as eager as I used to right at the start. But even then, I can’t say there was no pleasure at all. Sometimes it’s more like the enjoyment is quieter, or I notice it only afterward, or I’m not sure what I felt because I wasn’t paying close attention to that part.\n\nSo, no, I don’t have a specific example where I did cards or planning or something like that and it gave me no satisfaction when nothing else was interfering. So I’d say I haven’t really noticed that clearly.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you had times when activities you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, didn’t feel pleasurable or satisfying to you, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others or distracted by other thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m having trouble separating those things out, to be honest. When you say “even when you weren’t worried” and “not distracted by other thoughts,” that makes it harder for me to answer, because I don’t know that I’ve had many moments where everything else was completely out of the way and I could judge it cleanly.\n\nWith the event planning, there have been times where I did a task and it didn’t exactly feel satisfying in the old way, but I can’t say, “Oh, that was because the activity itself wasn’t pleasurable.” It might have just been that I was tired of thinking about it, or that I was doing it more out of habit. And with cards, I don’t think I’ve really tested it in a pure way, if that makes sense — like sitting down with no worries and no other thoughts and then noticing, “This isn’t fun.”\n\nSo I don’t think I can say yes in a definite way. I haven’t clearly noticed activities feeling flat or unsatisfying all by themselves. It’s more that I’m not sure what I’m feeling in the moment, and afterward I can’t always tell what part was enjoyment, what part was just getting through it, and what part was me being preoccupied.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any time in the past two weeks when you did something you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and you didn’t feel any pleasure or satisfaction from it, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others or thinking about other things?\n\nPatient: I can’t think of a clear time like that, no. I’m trying to be careful, because I don’t want to just say no too quickly if there was some moment I’m forgetting. But when I look back over the last couple weeks, I don’t remember doing something I normally enjoy and thinking, “There’s absolutely nothing here for me, no pleasure, no satisfaction,” especially if you’re asking me to leave out being worried or distracted.\n\nThere have been moments where I was doing something and I wasn’t exactly lively about it, if that makes sense. Like I might go through the motions a bit, or not feel as eager as I used to right at the start. But even then, I can’t say there was no pleasure at all. Sometimes it’s more like the enjoyment is quieter, or I notice it only afterward, or I’m not sure what I felt because I wasn’t paying close attention to that part.\n\nSo, no, I don’t have a specific example where I did cards or planning or something like that and it gave me no satisfaction when nothing else was interfering. I just haven’t noticed it in that clean a way.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, has there been a time when you did something you usually enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and afterward you thought to yourself, 'That didn’t feel good at all, even though I used to really like doing it'?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that exact thought afterward. I mean, I’ve looked back on things and wondered whether I enjoyed them as much as I used to, but I haven’t had a clear moment where I said to myself, “That didn’t feel good at all.”\n\nWith organizing things, sometimes I’m more focused on just getting through the steps, making sure I haven’t forgotten anything, and then afterward I might feel a little worn out rather than pleased. But that’s not the same as thinking I got no enjoyment from it. And with cards, I can’t say I’ve finished and thought, “Well, that used to be fun and now it’s nothing.” It’s more vague than that.\n\nSo if you’re asking whether I’ve noticed a definite loss of pleasure in something I usually like, I’d say no, not really. I haven’t noticed it clearly enough to say that.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you done something you used to really enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, and noticed that you didn’t care about it as much as you used to, even when you weren’t worried about how you might act around others or thinking about something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed that in a clear way, no. I mean, “didn’t care about it as much” is maybe a little closer than “got no pleasure,” because there are times I’m not as eager or I don’t throw myself into things the way I might have before. But if you’re asking whether I did something I usually enjoy and, when I wasn’t worried or distracted, I could tell I just didn’t care about it anymore or cared a lot less… I can’t really point to that.\n\nIt’s more muddled than that for me. Sometimes I start something and I’m sort of slow to get into it, or I do it because it’s what I normally do, and then later I’m not sure how much I cared in the moment. But I haven’t had a definite thought like, “This doesn’t matter to me now,” or “I don’t care about this the way I used to.” I’d be guessing if I said yes to that.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not that I’ve clearly noticed. I can say I’ve been uncertain about how I’m feeling during things, but I haven’t identified a specific time where the interest itself was plainly gone.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you found that activities you used to enjoy, like organizing events or playing cards, felt completely uninteresting or empty, even when you weren’t distracted by worries or other thoughts?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d put it that strongly, no. “Completely uninteresting or empty” doesn’t really fit what I’ve noticed. I mean, there are times lately where I’m not as sparked up about things as I might have been before, or I’m slower to settle into them, but I can’t honestly say I’ve sat there doing something I normally like and felt, “This is empty, there’s nothing in it for me.”\n\nAnd I know you’re making the point about not counting times when I’m distracted or worrying, and that’s part of why I’m hesitant to say yes. If my mind is elsewhere, then of course things don’t feel the same. I’m still not sure I can say yes to that. I know you’re asking it a little less strongly now—not “completely flat” or “empty,” but just not the same interest or enjoyment—and even with that, I’d have to be careful.

There are times when I don’t seem to jump into things the way I normally would, or I’ll be doing something and afterward I think, “Well, was I really enjoying that, or was I just doing it because it’s what I usually do?” But in the moment, when I try to sort out whether I actually had less interest versus just being a bit preoccupied or not paying attention to myself, I can’t really pin it down.

So I wouldn’t say I’ve clearly noticed that my interest or enjoyment is reduced in a definite way. It’s more that I’m uncertain about it. I don’t want to give you a yes just because I haven’t felt exactly like myself, because I can’t point to a clear example where I knew, without other stuff getting in the way, that I didn’t enjoy it the same.

What brings you in today? Is there something specific you'd like to talk about?\n\nPatient: Well… yes. I suppose what really pushed me to come in was something that happened when I was volunteering. It sounds small when I say it out loud, almost silly, but one of the younger volunteers had put a tablecloth on crooked, and I just— I snapped at him. Really snapped. Over a tablecloth.\n\nI don’t usually talk to people that way. I’ve always tried to be polite, especially with younger folks who are just trying to help, and afterward I felt embarrassed and kind of shaken by how quickly it came out of me. It wasn’t like me, and that’s what bothered me enough to think maybe I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of reaction—snapping or becoming very upset over small things—been happening more often lately, or was this a one-time occurrence?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s been happening all the time, no. The thing that really stands out—the reason I’m here—is that moment with the younger volunteer and the tablecloth. It was crooked, and I just blew up at him, much sharper than the situation called for.\n\nAnd the part that bothered me was how unlike me it felt. I don’t usually talk to people that way, especially not someone younger who was just trying to help. It wasn’t some big argument or anything important, which almost makes it worse in my mind.
